Which part of Bali is the quietest?Sidemen in the east, Munduk in the highlands, and the north coast around Lovina are the quieter regions.



===== one-base-or-two-in-bali [choosing-your-base / base-split] 1083w =====
One Base or Two? How to Split a Bali Stay
For most trips of a week or more, two bases in Bali work better than one. The island's beach areas and its cultural center are in different regions, and a single base means a long day trip to reach whichever one you did not choose. One base suits a short trip or a traveler who wants to unpack once. Two suit anyone who wants both the sea and Ubud.


## Why the question comes up

Bali's main attractions are split between the coast and the hills. The resort beaches are in the south. The rice terraces, temples and arts are around Ubud, which is about 22 miles from the airport to Ubud by road and about 20 from Seminyak. No single address puts both on your doorstep.


## The day-trip alternative

The alternative to a second base is a day trip. From a southern base, Ubud and back is a full day with a long drive at each end. We give no drive times, because they vary by hour. One such day is manageable. Three of them is a commute, and the reason people split their stay.

The three bases most trips choose between: Seminyak & Canggu · Ubud · Uluwatu & the Bukit. (Enable JavaScript for the interactive map.)


## The case for one base

One base has real advantages. You unpack once, you learn the neighborhood, and you lose no day to moving. It suits a trip of five nights or fewer, a traveler recovering from a long flight, and families with young children, for whom every hotel change is work.


## Choosing the single base

With one base, choose the one that matches the larger part of what you want. A trip mostly about beach, food and rest belongs in the south, with a day trip to Ubud. A trip mostly about culture and landscape belongs in Ubud, with a day at the coast, which is about 14 miles away at Sanur.


## What one base costs you

The cost of one base is whatever lies far from it. From Seminyak, the eastern temples and the mountain lakes are long days. From Ubud, a swim in the sea is a drive. Accept that plainly when you book, and resist the urge to fix it with back-to-back day trips.



## The case for two bases

Two bases let each part of the trip happen where it belongs. Beach days are spent at the beach, and temple and terrace days start a short ride from the temples and terraces. The transfer between the two takes part of one day, and it can include sightseeing stops on the way.


## What a move costs

A move costs a morning of packing, a drive and a check-in. Our view is that a move is worth it when you gain at least two full days in the new place. A base of two nights gives one full day, which is rarely enough to justify the change; three nights is our working minimum.


## Turning the transfer into a day out

The drive between bases is a chance to see what lies between them. A driver hired for the transfer can stop at a temple, a viewpoint or a lunch place on the way. That turns a moving day into a useful one and saves a separate outing.


## Three bases and more

A third base makes sense on a longer trip. With ten nights or more, a traveler can add one of the quieter regions: Sidemen or Amed in the east, Munduk in the highlands, or one of the Nusa islands. Each is far enough from the south that a day trip is impractical.


## When to stop adding

Every added base costs a moving day. Our advice is to count nights, not places: divide the nights you have by three, and treat the result as the upper limit on bases. A two-week trip with five bases is a trip spent in cars.


## Islands are a special case

An island base adds boat crossings, which depend on the sea. Travel guides give the crossing from Sanur to the Nusa islands as roughly 30 to 45 minutes. An island leg is a base with a weather-dependent link at each end, and it needs slack around it.


## How to decide for your trip

The decision between one base and two comes down to three questions. How many nights do you have? Do you want both the coast and the hills, or mainly one? And how much do you mind packing? These are our own suggestions for a starting point.


- Up to 5 nights: one base.

- 6 to 9 nights: two bases, one on the coast and one in Ubud.

- 10 nights or more: two or three bases.



## Travelers who should stay put

Some travelers are better off with one base whatever the length of the trip: families with small children, anyone with limited mobility, and those who came to rest. A day trip or two with a driver covers the highlights without a hotel change.


## Practical notes on splitting

Book a driver for the transfer day in advance and agree the stops and the price in writing. Check checkout and check-in times, since a gap between them is where the sightseeing stops fit. Confirm that each place is licensed accommodation, as Bali's rules for visitors require.


## What this decision actually sits inside

Notice what happened across all of this. One base or two was a question about nights, and nights were not the only thing in play. The order of the bases matters, since the first one follows a long flight and the last one precedes another. An island leg brings the sea into it. The split is where the whole shape of a trip gets decided.

How to order the bases, and how many nights to give each, is worked through in the complete Bali trip guide.



## FAQ

Should I stay in one place or two in Bali?For a trip of a week or more, two bases usually work better: one on the coast and one in Ubud. For five nights or fewer, stay in one.

How many nights should I spend in each base?Our working minimum is three nights per base, which gives two full days.

Can I see Ubud on a day trip from Seminyak?Yes. It is about 20 miles each way, and a full day with the drive. Several such days are a good reason to split the stay.

How do I get between bases in Bali?By car with a driver, who can stop at sights on the way. Agree the stops and the price in writing beforehand.



===== central-ubud-vs-the-outskirts [choosing-your-base / base-split] 1124w =====
Staying in Central Ubud vs. the Rice-Field Outskirts
Stay in central Ubud if you want to walk to restaurants, the market and the Monkey Forest, and stay in the surrounding villages if you want rice-field views and quiet. The center is convenient and congested; the outskirts are peaceful and need a ride for most errands. For a first visit of three nights, our view is that the edge of the center gives the best of both.


## What "Ubud" covers

Ubud looks like one town and is in fact fourteen villages, each with its own village committee. The center is built around three streets: Jalan Raya Ubud, Jalan Monkey Forest and Jalan Hanoman, which meet near the market and the palace. Everything else that carries the name Ubud on a booking site lies in the villages around that core.


## The villages by direction

To the north and west of the center are Campuhan, Sanggingan and Penestanan. To the south are Nyuh Kuning and Pengosekan. To the east is Peliatan. Further out, to the northeast, are the Tegallalang rice terraces, about 6 miles from the center. A listing in any of these may be described as Ubud.

The town and the terraces: Ubud · Tegallalang rice terraces. (Enable JavaScript for the interactive map.)


## Staying in the center

Central Ubud puts the town's sights within walking distance. The market, the palace, the museums and the Sacred Monkey Forest are all on or near the three main streets, along with most of the restaurants and shops. A visitor staying here can do without a car for whole days at a time.


## The drawback

The price of that convenience is traffic and crowds. Travel guides describe central Ubud as crowded with visitors and its traffic as very bad. The streets are narrow, and rooms that face them hear them. Ask whether a room looks onto the road or onto a garden behind; many central properties run back from the street into quiet courtyards.


## Who the center suits

The center suits first-time visitors, short stays, travelers without a driver, and anyone who wants to eat out at a different place each night. It suits those who do not ride a scooter, since a motorbike in Indonesia requires a license matching the type of vehicle driven.



## Staying in the outskirts

The villages around Ubud offer what most people picture when they imagine the town: a room looking over rice fields or a river valley, with birdsong in place of engines. Places here tend to have more space and gardens. The trade is distance from the restaurants and sights of the center.


## The drawback

Distance in Ubud is measured in rides, not miles. A property two miles from the center can mean a ride every time you want dinner, along lanes that are dark at night and have no sidewalk. Ask whether the property runs a shuttle to town, how often, and how late.


## Who the outskirts suit

The outskirts suit couples, honeymooners, longer stays, and anyone coming to Ubud for rest, yoga or writing. They suit travelers with a driver booked for their sightseeing days, since the car collects you wherever you are. They are a harder fit for a two-night stay with a long list of things to see.


## The middle option

The edge of the center is a third choice, and often the best one. A place a ten-minute walk from the main streets, in a village such as Penestanan or Nyuh Kuning, can look over fields and still be reachable on foot. We name no properties. Look at the walking route on a map, not only the distance.


## How to check a listing

These are our own suggestions for reading any Ubud listing.


- Find the exact location on a map and measure the walk to Jalan Raya Ubud.

- Check whether the route has a sidewalk and lighting.

- Ask about a shuttle, its hours, and whether it must be booked.

- Ask whether the room faces a road.



## Getting around from either

Getting around Ubud depends on where you sleep. From the center, walking covers the town, and a ride app or a driver covers the surroundings. From the outskirts, every trip into town is a ride. We have no official source on ride-app coverage in the villages, so ask your accommodation how guests usually get about.


## Day trips

For the sights outside town, the two locations are equal, since a driver collects you from either. From Ubud it is about 27 miles to Kintamani and about 22 to Sidemen. A driver booked for the day is the practical choice, with the route and price agreed in advance.


## Evenings

Evenings are where the choice shows. In the center, dinner is a walk away and so is the way home. In the outskirts, dinner means the property's own restaurant, a shuttle, or a ride arranged in both directions. Travelers who like to wander after dark should lean toward the center.


## Practical points for both

A few points apply wherever in Ubud you stay. Bali's rules require visitors to use licensed accommodation. Ubud is in Gianyar regency, where the local tax on hotel stays matches the 10% charged in the southern resorts, and a service charge may be added, so compare the final price.


## Climate and insects

Ubud sits at about 200 meters and is cooler than the coast. Open-sided rooms and rice-field settings are part of the appeal of the outskirts, and they come with mosquitoes. The CDC advises sleeping in air-conditioned or screened rooms, or under a bed net where the sleeping area is open to the outdoors.


## Monkeys

Properties near the Monkey Forest may receive visits from its residents. The CDC advises travelers in Indonesia to keep away from animals. Keep doors closed and food out of sight, and see a doctor promptly after any bite or scratch.


## How long, and in what order

The length of your stay tips the choice. For two or three nights, convenience matters most, and the center or its edge wins. For five nights or more, the quiet of the outskirts is worth the rides. Where Ubud falls in the order of a trip, and how many nights it deserves, is set out in the complete Bali trip guide.



## FAQ

Is it better to stay in central Ubud or outside?The center for walking to restaurants and sights; the outskirts for rice-field views and quiet. For a short first visit, the edge of the center is a good compromise.

Is central Ubud noisy?Travel guides describe the center as crowded with very bad traffic. Ask whether a room faces the road or a garden behind.

Can you walk around Ubud?The center is walkable, around three main streets. The surrounding villages usually need a ride.

Which villages are near Ubud?Campuhan, Sanggingan and Penestanan to the north and west, Nyuh Kuning and Pengosekan to the south, and Peliatan to the east.



===== short-term-rentals-in-bali-what-to-check [villas-and-resorts / base-split] 1056w =====
Short-Term Rentals in Bali: Licenses, Deposits and Fake Listings
Before booking a villa or short-term rental in Bali, check four things: that the property is licensed accommodation, that the listing is real, what the final price is once tax and service are added, and what the deposit and cancellation terms say. Bali's rules for visitors require a stay in licensed accommodation. Paying through a platform with a card protects you better than a bank transfer to a stranger.


## The license requirement

Bali's rules for foreign tourists, set out in the governor's circular of 2025, require visitors to stay in accommodation businesses that hold a license under the regulations. The rule is addressed to the visitor. An unlicensed room in a private house, however pleasant, is outside what the circular allows.


## How to check

We have no official public register to point you to, so the practical step is to ask. Request the property's business license details in writing before you book, and note the answer. A professionally run villa will answer without difficulty. Hesitation or a change of subject tells you something as well.


## Why it matters to you

The circular says violations by foreign tourists will be dealt with firmly under Indonesian law, and the US Consular Agency in Bali reports a current enforcement campaign by Indonesian authorities. We have no source on how the accommodation rule is enforced in practice, so we describe no penalty. The simple course is to stay somewhere that is licensed.


## Is the listing real?

A rental listing is a set of photographs and a promise. The State Department warns that financial scams happen in Indonesia and advises caution with anyone asking for money. The classic rental fraud is a listing copied from a real property, offered at a low price, with payment requested outside any platform.


## Signs to treat with caution

These are our own suggestions, not an official list.


- A price far below similar villas in the same area.

- A request to pay by bank transfer or cryptocurrency instead of through the platform.

- Pressure to decide today.

- No reviews, or reviews that all appeared in the same week.

- Photographs that turn up on other listings under another name.



## How to verify

Search for the property's name and photographs outside the listing. Look for it on a map and in street-level imagery. Ask the host for a short video call from the property. A real owner can show you the pool; someone using stolen photographs cannot.



## What the price really is

A villa's nightly rate is rarely the final figure. In Badung regency, which covers Seminyak, Canggu, Uluwatu and Nusa Dua, the local tax on accommodation is 10%, and a business may add a service charge. Platforms add their own fees. Compare properties on the total for your dates.


## What is included

Ask what the rate covers. Daily cleaning, breakfast, staff, electricity, pool maintenance and airport transfers are included in some rentals and charged separately in others. For longer stays, electricity in particular is sometimes billed by the meter. Get the list in writing.


## Currency

Bali's rules for visitors say transactions should be made in rupiah. If a price is quoted in dollars, ask what exchange rate applies on the day of payment. The State Department advises checking with your card issuer about international transaction fees.


## Deposits and cancellation

Deposits are where rental disputes begin. There are two kinds: a booking deposit, paid in advance to hold the dates, and a security deposit, held against damage. Ask how much each is, when it is taken, how it is returned and within how many days. We have no official source on local practice, so we describe none.


## The security deposit

On arrival, walk through the property with the manager and photograph anything already damaged, with the date visible. Do the same at departure. A written inventory, signed by both sides, is the best protection either of you has.


## Cancellation

Read the cancellation terms before paying. A long-haul trip from the United States has several ways to go wrong, and a non-refundable villa is a large sum to lose. Check whether your travel insurance covers cancellation and on what grounds. The State Department highly recommends travel insurance for Indonesia.


## Checking the property itself

A villa is a house, and the things that matter in a house matter here. Location comes first: look at what is within walking distance, since a villa down a long lane means a ride for every meal. Then ask about the practical matters a listing leaves out.


## Questions to ask


- Is the bedroom enclosed and air-conditioned, or open to the garden?

- Is there a fence or gate around the pool?

- How is drinking water supplied?

- Is there staff on site at night, and a phone number that is answered?

- How far is the nearest road a car can reach?



## Why those questions

Each has a reason. The CDC advises sleeping in air-conditioned or screened rooms to avoid mosquito bites. An unfenced pool is a hazard for children. The State Department says tap water in many areas of Indonesia is not safe to drink. And some villas are reached only by a footpath.


## Villa or hotel

A villa gives space, privacy and a private pool. A hotel gives a front desk, security, a restaurant and someone to call when something breaks. Our view is that a first-time visitor on a short trip is often better served by a hotel, and that villas come into their own for groups, families and longer stays.


## Where the villa is matters more than the villa

A beautiful villa in the wrong area is still in the wrong area. Which part of the island suits your trip comes before which property, and that choice is set out in the complete Bali trip guide.



## FAQ

Are short-term rentals legal in Bali?Bali's rules require foreign tourists to stay in licensed accommodation. Ask the property for its business license details before booking.

How do I avoid fake villa listings in Bali?Book through a platform, pay by card, be wary of prices far below the market, and verify the property on a map and by video call.

Is tax included in Bali villa prices?Not always. In Badung regency the local tax on accommodation is 10%, and a service charge may be added. Compare totals.

Should I pay a villa deposit by bank transfer?Our advice is to pay by card through a platform where you can. A transfer to an individual is hard to recover.



===== bali-with-kids [by-trip-type / base-split] 1139w =====
Bali with Kids: Bases, Car Seats and Pools
Bali works well with children when the trip is planned around a calm base, short drives and a pool. Sanur and Nusa Dua are the areas travel guides describe as suited to families. The matters that need thought in advance are car seats, which you should not expect to find, the water, the sun, mosquitoes and animals, and a first aid kit packed at home.


## Choosing a family base

A family base in Bali should be calm, close to the airport and easy to move around. Travel guides describe Sanur as a mature resort town that appeals mostly to families and older visitors, and Nusa Dua as an enclave of high-end resorts with very little traffic. Sanur is about 11 miles from the airport and Nusa Dua about 8.


## Sanur

Sanur is a town with a beach, on the east-facing coast. Its appeal for families is that it is quiet and walkable, with restaurants and shops at a small scale. It is also about 14 miles from Ubud, which makes a day trip to the rice terraces and temples realistic.


## Nusa Dua

Nusa Dua is built around large resorts. For a family that wants a kids' club, several pools and everything on site, it is the obvious choice. It is less a place to explore than a place to stay put, and meals are mostly taken in the resort.


## Ubud with children

Ubud suits older children who will enjoy rice terraces, temples and the Monkey Forest. It has no beach, and the town center is congested. A pool matters more here than on the coast. Our advice is to give Ubud two or three nights in the middle of a longer stay by the sea.



## Car seats and getting around

Car seats are the practical gap in a family trip to Bali. We have found no official source requiring child seats in cars there, and you should not expect a taxi, an app car or a driver's vehicle to carry one. If a seat matters to you, bring your own, or confirm one in writing with a driver before you travel.


## Use a driver

A car with a driver booked for the day is the family way to get around. Ask in advance about seat belts in the back seats and about fitting a child seat. Keep drives short: plan one outing a day and count the drive back. We give no drive times, since they vary by hour.


## Scooters

Families on scooters are a common sight in Bali and no model to follow. Indonesian law requires a helmet for every rider and passenger and a license matching the type of vehicle driven. Our advice is that children do not belong on a rented scooter.


## Water, food and stomachs

Children are the travelers most likely to swallow tap water without thinking. The State Department says tap water in many areas of Indonesia is not safe to drink and advises sealed bottled water. The CDC's advice is to brush teeth with bottled water and to avoid ice where water safety is in doubt.


## Habits to set on day one

Put a bottle of water by every sink. Teach children to keep their mouths closed in the shower and the pool. The CDC's food advice is to choose food that is cooked and served hot and to wash hands often with soap and water, especially before eating.


## If a child gets sick

The CDC's advice for travelers' diarrhea is to drink plenty of fluids and to use oral rehydration solution in serious cases. Pack rehydration salts suitable for children. Dehydration develops faster in a small body, so our advice is to see a doctor early with a child and not to wait it out.


## Sun, mosquitoes and animals

The three outdoor risks for children in Bali are sun, mosquitoes and animals. The CDC's advice on sun is to use sunscreen with an SPF of at least 15, wear protective clothing, and seek shade in the hottest part of the day, from 10 a.m. to 4 p.m.


## Mosquitoes

The CDC says mosquitoes in Indonesia spread diseases, including dengue, that cannot be prevented with a vaccine or medicine, and that the mosquitoes concerned bite by day as well as at night. Its advice is to cover skin, use an appropriate repellent as directed on the label, and sleep in air-conditioned or screened rooms.


## Animals

The CDC says dogs infected with rabies are commonly found in Indonesia and advises travelers to keep away from animals. Children are drawn to dogs, cats and monkeys. Set the rule before the trip: no touching any animal. Any bite or scratch needs washing and a doctor the same day.


## Health preparation

A family trip to Bali starts at the pediatrician. The CDC recommends that travelers be current on routine vaccines and recommends hepatitis A, hepatitis B and typhoid for Indonesia. Ask about each child's schedule well before departure, since some vaccines need time.


## Medicine

Bring children's medicines from home. The State Department warns that counterfeit medication is common in Indonesia. Prescription medicine should travel in its original packaging with the prescription, and over-the-counter medicine has its own small allowance at customs.


## Insurance and care

The State Department highly recommends travel insurance for Indonesia and says hospitals often require payment or a deposit upfront. Check that the policy covers every member of the family, and save the assistance number. Ask your hotel on arrival which clinic it would send a child to.


## Pools and the sea

A pool is the center of a family stay in Bali. Ask before booking whether a villa pool is fenced, since many are not. At the beach, we have no official source on sea conditions or lifeguards at any beach and call none safe. Ask the hotel where children can swim, and stay within reach.


## The flight and the first days

The journey from the United States is long, with at least one connection, and the time difference is 12 hours or more. Children adjust on their own schedule. Keep the first two days empty, expect early mornings, and use them at the pool.



## FAQ

Is Bali good for families with kids?Yes, with a calm base and short drives. Travel guides describe Sanur and Nusa Dua as the areas suited to families.

Are car seats available in Bali?Do not expect one in a taxi or a driver's car. Bring your own or confirm one in writing before you travel.

Can kids drink the water in Bali?No. The State Department says tap water in many areas of Indonesia is not safe to drink. Use sealed bottled water, including for brushing teeth.

What vaccines do children need for Bali?The CDC recommends being current on routine vaccines and recommends hepatitis A, hepatitis B and typhoid. Ask your pediatrician well ahead.



===== one-week-in-bali [by-trip-length / base-split] 1106w =====
One Week in Bali: A Realistic First-Timer Route
One week in Bali is enough for two areas done properly: Ubud for culture and rice terraces, and one beach base in the south. A realistic first-timer route is three nights in Ubud and four by the sea, or the reverse. Seven nights do not stretch to the north, the east and an island as well, and the best one-week trips are the ones that accept that.


## What a week can hold

A week in Bali, coming from the United States, is shorter than it looks. The journey has at least one connection and crosses 12 or more time zones, so the first day or two are spent adjusting. The CDC advises allowing at least two days before anything important. Plan five good days and treat the rest as recovery and travel.


## Two bases, not four

Bali's beach areas and its cultural center are about 20 miles apart by road, and the drive between them is slow. Our view is that two bases fit a week comfortably and three do not, since each move costs half a day. One coast base and Ubud cover what most first-time visitors come for.

A week, two bases: Seminyak & Canggu → Ubud → Uluwatu & the Bukit. (Enable JavaScript for the interactive map.)


## A realistic one-week route

The one-week route below gives a first-time visitor the two sides of Bali with one hotel change. It is a suggestion to adapt, with no drive times and no prices, since both vary. It assumes seven nights on the island and a morning or midday arrival.


- Day 1: arrive, transfer to Ubud, rest.

- Day 2: central Ubud on foot; an early night.

- Day 3: rice terraces and temples north of Ubud with a driver.

- Day 4: transfer to the coast, with a stop on the way.

- Days 5 and 6: beach days, with one outing.

- Day 7: a free day near your hotel.

- Day 8: depart.



## Days 1 to 3: Ubud

Ubud is about 22 miles from the airport to Ubud by road. Spend the first full day on foot in the center: the market, the palace and the Sacred Monkey Forest. On the second, hire a driver for the Tegallalang rice terraces, about 6 miles north, and a temple such as Tirta Empul.


## Day 4: the transfer

The move from Ubud to the coast takes part of a day. A driver hired for the transfer can stop on the way, which turns a moving day into a sightseeing one. Agree the stops and the price in writing beforehand, and arrive at the beach in time for the sunset.


## Days 5 to 7: the coast

Which coast base depends on what you want. Seminyak suits dining and beach walks, Canggu surfing, the Bukit peninsula cliffs and quiet, and Sanur a calm stay with children. Give these days to the sea, with one outing: Uluwatu Temple from a southern base, or Tanah Lot from Canggu.



## Ubud first or beach first?

The order of the two bases is a real choice. Starting in Ubud puts the sightseeing early, while jet lag has you awake at dawn, and ends the trip with rest by the sea, close to the airport. Starting at the beach gives an easy landing after the flight and ends the trip inland, about 22 miles from the terminal.


## Our preference

Our view is that Ubud first works better for most first-time visitors. Early mornings suit temples and rice terraces, and the last night falls near the airport. A traveler who arrives late at night may prefer the reverse, to avoid a drive inland in the dark after a long journey.


## What to leave out

A one-week trip is defined by what it omits. The north coast and Munduk are about 51 miles or more from the airport. Amed, in the east, is about 61. The Nusa islands need boat crossings that depend on the sea. Each of these deserves its own nights, and a week cannot spare them.


## The Mount Batur question

The sunrise trek on Mount Batur is the one long outing that fits a week, because Kintamani is about 27 miles from Ubud. It starts in the middle of the night. Do it on your second or third Ubud morning, never on the first, and keep the rest of that day free.


## The island day-trip question

A day trip to Nusa Penida from the south is possible in a week, at the cost of a full day and two boat crossings. Our advice is to choose between that and a rest day, and to place it mid-trip. A boat the day before a long-haul flight is a risk with nothing to gain.


## Practical points for a week

A few rules bear on any one-week plan. An American needs a tourist visa, at IDR 500,000, and pays Bali's tourist levy of IDR 150,000 per person. The arrival card can only be filled in during the 72 hours before landing.


## Check the calendar

Check your week against Nyepi, the day of silence, which in 2027 falls on Monday, March 8, 2027. Press reports of the 2026 arrangements describe regular flights halting for 24 hours. A day lost that way is a seventh of a one-week trip.


## Getting around

Use a driver for the day trips and the transfer, and ride apps for short hops in the south. A scooter requires a license that covers motorcycles under Indonesian law, which most Americans do not hold.


## What this decision actually sits inside

Notice what happened across all of this. The route was simple, and almost every line of it depended on something outside the route: the hour your flight lands, the month you travel, whether your coast base suits the season, and how far the last night is from the airport. A day-by-day plan is the visible part. The choices underneath it are what make it work or not.

How to fit those choices to your own dates and flights is worked through in the complete Bali trip guide.



## FAQ

Is one week enough for Bali?Yes, for two areas: Ubud and one beach base. It is not enough to add the north, the east and an island as well.

How should I split a week in Bali?Our suggestion is three nights in Ubud and four on the coast, with one hotel change.

Should I go to Ubud first or the beach first?Our view is Ubud first for most visitors, so the last night is near the airport. Late-night arrivals may prefer the beach first.

Can I visit Nusa Penida on a one-week trip?As a day trip from the south, yes, at the cost of a full day. Place it mid-trip, not the day before your flight.



===== ten-days-in-bali [by-trip-length / boat-day-buffer] 1033w =====
Ten Days in Bali: Adding the Nusa Islands
Ten days in Bali gives room for a third stop, and for most travelers the best use of it is a few nights on the Nusa islands. A workable shape is three nights in Ubud, three on Nusa Lembongan or Nusa Penida, and four at a beach base in the south. The island leg belongs in the middle, with the boat well clear of the flight home.


## What ten days adds

Ten nights are three more than a week, and those three nights are what make a third base possible. A week covers Ubud and one coast base. Ten days add one more region without rushing the first two. The Nusa islands, off the southeast coast, are the closest addition, since boats leave from Sanur.


## Why the islands

The three Nusa islands offer what the mainland's south does not: small-island days with very little traffic, cliffs, snorkeling and surf. Nusa Lembongan is about 8 square kilometers and nearly free of cars. Nusa Penida is far larger and wilder. Tiny Nusa Ceningan is joined to Lembongan by a bridge.

Ten days, three bases: Seminyak & Canggu → Ubud → Sidemen → Amed → Uluwatu & the Bukit. (Enable JavaScript for the interactive map.)


## A ten-day route

The ten-day route below adds an island leg to the classic pairing of Ubud and the coast. It is a suggestion to adapt, with no prices and no drive times. It assumes ten nights and puts the boat crossings on days four and seven, away from both flights.


- Days 1 to 3: Ubud.

- Day 4: drive to Sanur, boat to Nusa Lembongan.

- Days 5 and 6: Lembongan, Ceningan and a day on Nusa Penida.

- Day 7: boat back to Sanur, transfer to a southern beach base.

- Days 8 to 10: the coast.

- Day 11: depart.



## Days 1 to 3: Ubud

Ubud comes first, while jet lag wakes you early. It is about 22 miles from the airport. Walk the center on the first full day and take a driver to the rice terraces and temples on the second. Ubud is also about 13 miles from Sanur's harbor, which makes it a convenient place to leave from.


## Days 4 to 6: the islands

Boats to the Nusa islands leave mainly from Sanur. Travel guides give the crossing as roughly 30 to 45 minutes and note that most boats to Lembongan involve a wet landing, wading from the beach. Stay on Lembongan and visit Penida from there; guides put that hop at about ten minutes by local boat.


## Days 7 to 10: the coast

Return to Bali with three or four nights left, and spend them at a beach base that suits you: Seminyak, Canggu, the Bukit or Sanur itself. Sanur has the advantage of needing no transfer after the boat. These last days carry no boats and no long drives.



## Why the boat goes in the middle

The island leg sits in the middle of this route for one reason. Boat crossings depend on sea conditions, and we have no official source on how often they are delayed or cancelled. A boat on day seven leaves several days of slack before the flight. A boat on day ten leaves none.


## What travel guides say about boats

Travel guides advise against boarding any fast boat you have doubts about, whether because of the vessel, overloading or the weather. Take a morning boat, in daylight. Keep phones and passports in a dry bag, and carry luggage you can lift, since a wet landing means wading with it.


## Lembongan or Penida for the nights

The two main islands suit different travelers. Lembongan is small, easy and built for visitors, with a choice of places to stay. Penida is about 247 square kilometers, with rough roads and simpler services. Our view is that Lembongan is the better base for a first island stay.


## What to do there

Two full days on the islands cover a snorkeling trip, the cliffs of Lembongan's south coast, the bridge to Ceningan, and a day on one side of Penida. Travel guides warn of rip currents at Dream Beach and of fatal falls at Devil's Tear; stay behind the barriers.


## Alternatives to the island leg

The islands are one way to use three extra nights, and not the only one. A traveler uneasy on boats can add East Bali instead: Sidemen is about 22 miles from Ubud, and Amed about 49. A traveler who wants mountains can add Munduk, about 39 miles from Ubud.


## Choosing among them

These are our own suggestions. Choose the islands for sea, snorkeling and beaches. Choose Sidemen for rice terraces and walking without crowds. Choose Amed for diving from the shore. Choose Munduk for waterfalls and cool air. Each replaces the island leg in the middle of the route.


## Practical points

A ten-night stay is well inside the 30 days the tourist visa allows. The visa costs IDR 500,000 and Bali's tourist levy is IDR 150,000 per person. Bring cash to the islands and any medicine you rely on, since both are harder to come by there.


## Insurance

The State Department strongly recommends insurance that covers medical evacuation in Indonesia, and says an injured traveler will likely need to reach a major city for care. From an island, that begins with a boat. Check how your policy treats boat travel, snorkeling and motorbikes.


## Where the slack goes

Ten days leave room for one spare day, and where you put it matters more than whether you have it. How the island leg, the spare day and the departure are spaced is set out in the complete Bali trip guide.



## FAQ

Is 10 days enough for Bali?Yes. Ten nights cover Ubud, a beach base and one more region, such as the Nusa islands.

How should I split 10 days in Bali?Our suggestion is three nights in Ubud, three on the Nusa islands and four at a southern beach base.

Should I stay on Nusa Lembongan or Nusa Penida?Our view is Lembongan for a first island stay, with a day visit to Penida, which is about ten minutes away by local boat.

When in the trip should I do the islands?In the middle. Boats depend on the sea, so keep several days between the return crossing and your flight home.



===== two-weeks-in-bali [by-trip-length / base-split] 1131w =====
Two Weeks in Bali: North, East and an Island Leg
Two weeks in Bali is enough to leave the south behind for part of the trip. With fourteen nights, a traveler can pair Ubud and a beach base with two of the quieter regions: the central highlands around Munduk, East Bali at Sidemen or Amed, or the Nusa islands. A sensible shape is four bases of three to four nights, moving in one direction around the island.


## What two weeks makes possible

Fourteen nights change the arithmetic of a Bali trip. A week covers two areas and ten days three. Two weeks cover four, which is enough to include regions that are too far from the airport for a short stay: Munduk is about 51 miles away by road, and Amed about 61.


## The rule of three nights

Our working rule is at least three nights per base, which gives two full days in each. Fourteen nights divided that way gives four bases. A fifth is possible and makes the trip feel like a relay. Our view is that four well-chosen bases beat six hurried ones.

Two weeks, with the north and the islands: Seminyak & Canggu → Ubud → Munduk & the north → Sidemen → Amed → Nusa Lembongan → Uluwatu & the Bukit. (Enable JavaScript for the interactive map.)


## A two-week route

The two-week route below makes a loop through the center, the highlands and the east before finishing on the coast. It is a suggestion to adapt, with no prices and no drive times. Each move is a half-day drive with a driver, and the distances between bases are short in miles.


- Nights 1 to 4: Ubud.

- Nights 5 to 7: Munduk and the central highlands.

- Nights 8 to 10: East Bali, at Sidemen or Amed.

- Nights 11 to 14: a beach base in the south.



## Nights 1 to 4: Ubud

Ubud opens the trip, about 22 miles from the airport. Four nights allow for jet lag, a day on foot in the center, a day of rice terraces and temples, and a sunrise trek on Mount Batur for those who want it, since Kintamani is about 27 miles away.


## Nights 5 to 7: Munduk

Munduk is about 39 miles from Ubud by road, and the drive passes the mountain lakes and the temple of Ulun Danu Bratan. Travel guides describe the area as known for waterfalls, coffee and clove plantations and walking, at more than 700 meters of altitude. Pack a sweater.


## Nights 8 to 10: the east

From the highlands the route turns east. This is the longest transfer of the trip, about 93 miles to Amed, and it deserves an early start. Sidemen, a valley of rice terraces under Mount Agung, is the alternative for travelers who prefer walking to snorkeling.


## Nights 11 to 14: the coast

The trip ends by the sea, close to the airport. From Amed it is about 51 miles to Sanur. Four nights here are for rest, a beach and a last temple visit, with no long drives and no boats before the flight home.



## Swapping in an island leg

The Nusa islands can replace either the highlands or the east in this route. Boats leave from Sanur, and travel guides give the crossing as roughly 30 to 45 minutes. An island leg fits best between Ubud and the final coast base, where a delayed boat costs a beach day and nothing more.


## Which to swap out

These are our own suggestions. Keep the highlands and swap the east for the islands if you want mountains and sea. Keep the east and swap the highlands for the islands if diving and snorkeling are the point of the trip. Doing all three is possible and leaves little time anywhere.


## Why the route runs one way

The order of this route follows the map. Ubud, Munduk, the east and the south form a rough circle, so each drive covers new ground and none doubles back through the busy south. A route that zigzags, from Ubud to Uluwatu and back north to Munduk, spends days on the road.


## Transfers as sightseeing

Each transfer is a chance to see what lies between bases. A driver hired for the day can stop at a temple, a waterfall or a viewpoint. Agree the stops and the price in writing beforehand. This is how a two-week trip sees the island without separate day trips.


## Start inland, end at the sea

Beginning in Ubud and ending on the coast has two advantages. The early waking of jet lag is put to use among temples and terraces, and the last night falls close to the airport. Reversing the route is workable for travelers who want the beach first.


## What two weeks still cannot do

Two weeks are generous and still finite. The far west of the island, with its national park, is a long drive from everything else. The Gili Islands and Lombok need two travel days of their own. Our advice is to leave both for another trip unless one of them is the reason you are coming.


## Leave a spare day

A fourteen-night plan with every day assigned is fragile. Rain, a stomach bug or a road closed for a ceremony will take a day from somewhere. Our advice is to leave one day unplanned in the second half, where it can absorb whatever happens.


## Practical points for a longer stay

Two weeks are inside the 30 days the tourist visa allows, so no extension is needed. The visa costs IDR 500,000. Check the trip against the calendar: Nyepi falls on Monday, March 8, 2027, and Galungan on January 13 and August 11 that year.


## Season

Season shapes this route more than a shorter one, since it visits mountains and two coasts. South Bali's climate record shows about 1 inch of rain in July and about 14 in January. The mountains have their own weather, and the east-facing and west-facing coasts trade places for surf between the seasons.


## Medical care and cash

The State Department says adequate medical care may be harder to find in rural areas of Indonesia. Carry the medicine you rely on and enough cash for the quieter regions. How the bases, the transfers and the spare day are ordered for your dates is set out in the complete Bali trip guide.



## FAQ

Is two weeks too long for Bali?No. Fourteen nights allow four bases, including quieter regions such as Munduk and East Bali that a shorter trip cannot reach.

How many places should I stay in two weeks in Bali?Our suggestion is four bases of three to four nights each.

What is a good two-week Bali route?Ubud, then Munduk in the highlands, then East Bali at Sidemen or Amed, finishing at a beach base in the south.

Do I need a visa extension for two weeks in Bali?No. The tourist visa allows a stay of 30 days.



===== how-many-days-in-bali [by-trip-length / long-haul-arrival] 1061w =====
How Many Days Do You Need in Bali, Coming from the US?
Coming from the United States, plan on at least ten days in Bali, and treat a week as the minimum worth the journey. The trip involves at least one connection each way and a time difference of 12 to 16 hours, so two days are lost to travel and two more to adjusting. Seven nights give about five good days; ten give eight; fourteen give a proper trip.


## Why the answer is different for Americans

The number of days Bali needs depends on where you start. There is no nonstop flight from the United States, so every itinerary has a connection. Bali is 12 or 13 hours ahead of New York and 15 or 16 ahead of Los Angeles. A visitor from Australia or Singapore can make a long weekend of it; an American cannot.


## The travel days

The journey out crosses the International Date Line, so the calendar jumps ahead on the way to Bali. Depending on the routing, a traveler leaving on a Friday evening can land on Sunday. The return gives a day back on the calendar. Either way, a trip described as "a week" on the vacation request is shorter on the ground.


## The adjustment days

The CDC says jet lag can affect mood, concentration and physical performance, and advises arriving at least two days before any important event. With day and night nearly reversed, the first two days in Bali are best kept light. They count as days on the island and deliver less than the later ones.

What each extra block of days buys: Seminyak & Canggu · Ubud · Uluwatu & the Bukit · Nusa Penida. (Enable JavaScript for the interactive map.)


## What each length gives you

The useful way to compare trip lengths is by good days on the ground, after travel and adjustment are taken out. The figures below are our own estimate, not a measurement. They assume the first two days are slow and the last day is partly spent leaving.


- 5 nights: about three good days. One base only.

- 7 nights: about five good days. Two bases.

- 10 nights: about eight good days. Three bases.

- 14 nights: about twelve good days. Four bases.



## Five nights

Five nights is too short for most Americans. The long-haul flight is the largest fixed cost of the trip, and it buys three good days. Our view is that five nights make sense only as part of a longer Asian trip, when you are already in the region and the time zone.


## Seven nights

A week is the practical minimum. It covers Ubud and one beach base with one hotel change. It leaves no room for the north, the east or an island stay, and no slack if a day is lost to weather or illness. Travelers with only a week should plan a simple trip.



## Ten days and two weeks

Ten nights is the length we recommend for a first trip from the United States. It gives room for a third stop, such as a few nights on the Nusa islands, and one spare day. Fourteen nights add a fourth base, which opens the central highlands and East Bali.


## The case for ten

Ten nights spread the cost and effort of the flight over enough days to justify it. They allow three nights in each of three places, the minimum we suggest for a base. By our estimate, ten nights is also roughly what two weeks off work turns into once the travel days and a day to recover at home are counted.


## The case for fourteen

Two weeks suit travelers who want to see beyond the south. Munduk is about 51 miles from the airport and Amed about 61; neither works as a day trip. With fourteen nights you can stay in such places instead of driving to them.


## Longer stays

Stays of three or four weeks are common among remote workers and retirees, and Bali rewards them. The tourist visa allows a stay of 30 days, extendable once for another 30 days. An extension costs IDR 500,000 per application and requires a visit to an immigration office.


## Work and the tourist visa

A longer stay raises a question a short one does not. The tourist visa does not allow selling goods or services, or being paid by anyone in Indonesia. Anyone planning to work while in Bali should get proper advice on the right visa before traveling.


## How to decide

The right length for your trip follows from three things: how much time off you have, what you want to see, and how you handle long flights. Start from the time off, subtract a day at each end for travel, and subtract one more at home before work. What remains is your nights in Bali.


## If you have one week off

One week off work gives about six nights in Bali from most of the United States. Our advice is to keep it to one or two bases and to accept a narrow trip, or to wait until you can take longer. A rushed week at the far end of a very long flight is hard to enjoy.


## If you have two weeks off

Two weeks off gives ten to twelve nights, which is the comfortable range. Use the weekends at both ends. Leave a day at home before returning to work, since the CDC's two-day guideline for jet lag applies to your first meeting as much as to your first temple.


## What the length does not settle

The number of days is the first decision and not the last. Two trips of ten nights can be very different, depending on where the nights are spent and in what order. How to turn a number of nights into a set of bases is set out in the complete Bali trip guide.



## FAQ

How many days do you need in Bali from the US?At least ten nights for a first trip. A week is the practical minimum, since travel and jet lag take about four days in all.

Is 5 days in Bali worth it from the US?Rarely. Five nights give about three good days after travel and adjustment, unless you are already in Asia.

Is a week in Bali enough?Enough for Ubud and one beach base. It leaves no room for the north, the east or an island stay.

How long can Americans stay in Bali?Thirty days on the tourist visa, extendable once for another 30 days.



===== bali-honeymoon-how-to-split-the-nights [by-trip-type / base-split] 1161w =====
Bali Honeymoon: How to Split the Nights
The best split for a Bali honeymoon is two or three bases, starting somewhere quiet to recover from the wedding and the flight, and ending by the sea near the airport. For ten nights, a split that works is four nights around Ubud, three on an island or in East Bali, and three at a beach base. Fewer moves and longer stays suit a honeymoon better than a tour.


## What a honeymoon needs from a route

A honeymoon differs from an ordinary vacation in what it is for. Most couples arrive tired, from the wedding and from a journey with at least one connection across 12 or more time zones. The trip should begin with rest, hold its adventures for the middle, and end without a rush for the plane.


## Fewer bases, longer stays

By our estimate, a hotel change costs about half a day of packing and driving. Our view is that a honeymoon of ten nights should have three bases at most, and that two is often better. Three nights in a place is our working minimum, and four or five in the first one lets the trip start slowly.

Inland first, coast second: Ubud → Uluwatu & the Bukit. (Enable JavaScript for the interactive map.)


## Where to start

The first base should be the calmest. The CDC advises allowing at least two days for jet lag before anything important, and after a wedding the need is greater. The countryside around Ubud suits this well: a villa or small hotel among rice fields, cooler than the coast, with nothing that has to be done.


## Ubud's villages

Ubud is made up of fourteen villages, and the quieter ones lie around the center. Travel guides describe the town center as crowded and congested. A place outside it offers privacy and views, at the cost of a ride into town for dinner; ask about a shuttle.


## What to do in the first days

Do little. Our advice is a massage, a walk through the fields and one early dinner out. On the third day, hire a driver for the rice terraces and a temple. Jet lag wakes Americans early in Bali, and early mornings among the terraces are the best use of it.



## The middle of the trip

The middle nights are for the adventurous part, when you are rested and the flight home is still far off. The choice is between an island and the quieter side of the mainland. Each gives a change of scene from Ubud, and each is far enough to need its own nights.


## An island

Nusa Lembongan lies off the southeast coast, roughly 30 to 45 minutes from Sanur by boat according to travel guides. It is small, nearly free of cars, and suited to snorkeling and long lunches. Travel guides note that most boats involve a wet landing, wading from the beach, so pack light for this leg.


## East Bali

Couples who would sooner avoid boats can go east. Sidemen, about 22 miles from Ubud, is a valley of rice terraces below Mount Agung, described by travel guides as one of the most scenic parts of the island. Amed, about 49 miles from Ubud, offers snorkeling from the beach and very quiet evenings.


## The highlands

A third possibility is Munduk, about 39 miles from Ubud, at more than 700 meters, with waterfalls and cool air. Our view is that it suits couples who like walking and do not mind a sweater in the evening.


## Where to finish

The last base should be by the sea and close to the airport. The Bukit peninsula, with Uluwatu about 13 miles from the terminal, offers cliff views and privacy. Seminyak, about 7 miles away, offers restaurants and beach walks. Nusa Dua, about 8 miles away, offers large resorts with everything on site.


## Choosing the final base

These are our own suggestions. Choose the Bukit for seclusion and scenery. Choose Seminyak if you want to dress up and eat well. Choose Nusa Dua or Sanur for calm and ease. All three keep the drive to the airport short on the last morning.


## Keep boats out of the last days

Whatever the middle of the trip holds, come back to the mainland with days to spare. Boats depend on the sea, and we have no official source on how often they are delayed. A honeymoon is a poor trip on which to test the margin before an international flight.


## Splits by length

The split of a Bali honeymoon depends on how many nights you have. These are our own suggestions, with no prices. Each one starts with the longest stay near Ubud, puts any boat in the middle, and ends at a beach base near the airport.


- 7 nights: 4 near Ubud, 3 at the beach.

- 10 nights: 4 near Ubud, 3 on an island or in the east, 3 at the beach.

- 14 nights: 4 near Ubud, 3 in the highlands or east, 3 on an island, 4 at the beach.



## When to go

South Bali's climate record shows the driest months as July and August, at about 1 inch of rain, and the wettest as December to February. May, June and September have low rainfall and fall outside the northern summer peak. A honeymoon that follows a summer wedding lands in the dry season.


## Practical matters couples forget

A few practical points matter more on a honeymoon than they seem to. If either of you has changed a name, the flight booking, the passport and the visa must all match the passport you travel on. The passport must be valid for 6 months beyond your arrival date and have 2 blank pages.


## Insurance and health

The State Department highly recommends travel insurance for Indonesia and strongly recommends cover for medical evacuation. Anyone who is pregnant or planning a pregnancy should read the CDC's current Level 2 travel health notice for Zika in Bali and talk to a doctor before booking.


## Villas

Bali's rules require visitors to stay in licensed accommodation. Ask a villa for its license details, check whether the bedroom is enclosed and air-conditioned, and compare prices with tax and service included. How to order the bases around your flights is set out in the complete Bali trip guide.



## FAQ

How many days do you need for a Bali honeymoon?Ten nights is a comfortable length from the United States, allowing three bases of three to four nights each.

Where should we stay on a Bali honeymoon?Our suggestion is to start in the countryside around Ubud, spend the middle on an island or in East Bali, and finish by the sea near the airport.

What is the best month for a Bali honeymoon?May, June and September have low rainfall in the south Bali climate record and fall outside the July and August peak.

Is Bali safe for a honeymoon if we are planning a pregnancy?The CDC has a Level 2 travel health notice for Zika in Bali. Read the current notice and talk to a doctor before booking.



===== balinese-food-what-to-order [balinese-food / None] 1155w =====
Balinese Food: What to Order and What Is in It
Balinese food is its own cuisine within Indonesia, built on pork, duck, chicken, seafood and vegetables, seasoned with a spice paste of chilies, shallots, garlic, ginger and turmeric. The dishes to try first are babi guling, the spit-roasted pig; bebek betutu, slow-cooked spiced duck; sate lilit, minced meat on lemongrass; and nasi campur, a plate of rice with a little of everything.


## What makes Balinese food different

Balinese cooking differs from the food of the rest of Indonesia mainly in its use of pork. Bali is a Hindu island in a mostly Muslim country, and pork is central to its cuisine, while beef is rarely eaten because of Hindu tradition. Chicken, duck, seafood and vegetables fill out the table.


## The spice paste

Most Balinese dishes begin with a spice paste, called base genep or basa gede. It combines garlic, shallots, red chilies, ginger, turmeric and palm sugar, and it usually contains shrimp paste. That last ingredient matters to anyone with a shellfish allergy, since it is ground into the base of a dish and cannot be seen.


## Other building blocks

Coconut is everywhere, as grated flesh, as coconut milk and as oil. Peanuts appear in sauces. Chilies give most dishes some heat. Rice comes with nearly every meal. A diner who knows these five things, the paste, coconut, peanuts, chili and rice, can read most of a Balinese menu.


## The dishes to order

A first-time visitor can cover the heart of Balinese cooking with a handful of dishes. None of them is hard to find. We name no restaurants and quote no prices; ask your accommodation where local people go for each one, since the best version of a dish is often at a place that makes little else.


## Babi guling

Babi guling is spit-roasted suckling pig, stuffed with chili, turmeric, garlic and ginger before roasting. It is Bali's best-known dish. A serving typically comes as a plate of rice with slices of meat, crisp skin and several side preparations. It is a pork dish through and through.


## Bebek betutu and ayam betutu

Betutu is duck or chicken rubbed heavily with spice paste, wrapped in leaves and cooked slowly until the meat falls apart. Bebek is duck and ayam is chicken. Because of the long cooking, some places ask for the dish to be ordered in advance, so ask when you book.


## Sate lilit

Sate lilit is spiced minced meat pressed around a stick of lemongrass and grilled. It differs from the satay Americans may know, which is chunks of meat on a wooden skewer with peanut sauce. The mince can be pork, chicken or fish, so ask which if it matters to you.


## Nasi campur

Nasi campur means mixed rice. It is a plate of rice with small portions of several things: meat or fish, tofu, tempeh, vegetables and a spoonful of sambal. The Balinese version is a good first meal, because it shows you a range of flavors on one plate.



## What is in it: dishes to ask about

Some Balinese dishes contain ingredients a visitor would not guess. Lawar is the main example. It is a mix of finely chopped vegetables, such as young jackfruit, with grated coconut, minced meat and pork rind. The red version contains fresh pig's blood; the white version does not.


## Lawar

Lawar often arrives as a side on a babi guling or nasi campur plate, without being ordered separately. Anyone who avoids blood, pork or raw ingredients should ask what the lawar is made of. The question is ordinary, and a cook will answer it without offense.


## Sambal

Sambal is the chili relish served with most meals. Sambal matah is the Balinese kind: raw shallots, chili, lemongrass and lime in coconut oil. It is fresh and sharp, and it is hot. If you do not eat spicy food, "tidak pedas" means not spicy, and it is worth saying when you order.


## Urutan and other pork

Urutan is a Balinese pork sausage. Pork also turns up as rind in vegetable dishes and as stock. Travelers who do not eat pork should say "Saya tidak makan babi", I do not eat pork, and should not assume that a vegetable dish is free of it.


## Vegetables, sweets and drinks

Balinese cooking has plenty beyond meat. Jukut urab is a dish of vegetables tossed with grated coconut and spices. Tofu and tempeh, both made from soybeans, are everyday foods across Indonesia and appear on most rice plates. Vegetarians eat well in Bali, with one caution about the spice paste.


## The vegetarian caution

A dish with no visible meat may still be built on a paste containing shrimp paste, or cooked in a meat stock. A strict vegetarian or vegan should ask. "Saya vegetarian" is understood, and "tanpa" means without. Restaurants that cater to visitors are used to the question.


## Sweets

Laklak are small rice-flour pancakes served with grated coconut and palm sugar syrup. Bubuh injin is a pudding of black sticky rice with coconut milk. Both are sold as snacks and breakfast, and both are sweet and mild after a spicy meal.


## Drinks

Brem is a Balinese rice wine. Arak is a distilled spirit, and the State Department warns of drink poisoning in Indonesia from alcohol mixed with methanol. Our advice is to drink spirits only from a sealed, labeled bottle. Drink bottled water, and ask for no ice where you are unsure.


## Eating safely

The CDC's advice for travelers in this part of the world is to eat food that is cooked and served hot, to avoid food that has been sitting out on a buffet, and to wash hands often. The State Department says tap water in many areas of Indonesia is not safe to drink.


## Where to eat

Small local eating houses, called warungs, are where much of this food is served. The CDC advises caution with street vendors and says the same food safety rules apply if you eat there. Our advice is to choose a busy place where food is cooked to order and arrives hot.


## The bill

In Badung regency, which covers Seminyak, Canggu and Uluwatu, the local tax on restaurant food and drink is 10%, and a restaurant may add a service charge. A simple local eating house may include everything in the listed price; look for a line at the foot of the menu.



## FAQ

What is the most famous food in Bali?Babi guling, spit-roasted suckling pig stuffed with chili, turmeric, garlic and ginger.

Is Balinese food spicy?Most dishes have some chili heat, and sambal is hot. "Tidak pedas" means not spicy.

Is Balinese food halal?Pork is central to Balinese cooking, so many dishes are not. Ask at each place, and say "Saya tidak makan babi" if you do not eat pork.

What is lawar made of?Chopped vegetables such as young jackfruit with coconut, minced meat and pork rind. The red version contains fresh pig's blood.



===== food-allergies-and-dietary-needs-in-bali [balinese-food / health-chain] 1159w =====
Food Allergies and Dietary Needs in Bali: How to Ask
Eating in Bali with a food allergy or dietary restriction is manageable and needs more care than at home. The main risks are hidden ingredients: shrimp paste ground into spice bases, peanuts in sauces, and pork in dishes that look vegetarian. Carry a written card in Indonesian, say "Saya alergi akan" followed by the food, bring your own medication, and know where you would go in an emergency.


## Why allergies are harder here

Food allergies are harder to manage in Bali because the ingredients that cause trouble are often invisible. Balinese cooking starts from a spice paste that usually contains shrimp paste. Peanuts are used in sauces. Coconut milk is in many dishes. None of these is listed on a menu, and a cook may not think to mention them.


## No labeling you can rely on

We have found no official source on allergen labeling rules for restaurants in Indonesia, so we describe none. Our advice is to assume there is no labeling, to ask every time, and to treat a confident "no problem" with the same caution as no answer at all.


## Cross-contact

A small kitchen uses the same oil, the same wok and the same mortar for many dishes. A dish made without peanuts may still have touched them. For someone with a severe allergy, that matters more than the recipe. Tell the staff the allergy is serious and ask whether the dish can be cooked separately.


## The common hidden ingredients

Four ingredients account for most surprises in Balinese food: shrimp paste, peanuts, pork and soy. Each is ordinary in the local kitchen, and each can be present in a dish that gives no sign of it. Knowing where they hide is most of the work.


## Shellfish and shrimp paste

The basic Balinese spice paste usually includes shrimp paste, and it is the foundation of many meat, fish and vegetable dishes. A plate with no seafood on it can still contain shellfish. Anyone allergic to shellfish should ask about the paste in every cooked dish, and "udang" is the word for shrimp.


## Peanuts and nuts

Peanuts appear in sauces, most obviously with satay and salads. The Indonesian word "kacang" covers peanuts and is also used for other nuts and for beans, so the word alone can cause confusion. Be specific, and show a picture if you can. Fried foods may be cooked in shared oil.


## Pork

Pork is central to Balinese cooking, as meat, as sausage, as rind mixed into vegetable dishes and as stock. Travelers who avoid pork for religious or other reasons should say so plainly: "Saya tidak makan babi" means I do not eat pork. Do not assume a vegetable dish is free of it.


## Soy, gluten and dairy

Tofu and tempeh are made from soybeans, and soy sauce is widely used in Indonesian cooking. Anyone avoiding gluten should ask about soy sauce, which is commonly brewed with wheat. Dairy is uncommon in traditional Balinese dishes, where coconut milk does the same work, and common in cafes that cater to visitors.



## How to ask

Asking clearly, in Indonesian, is the most useful thing a traveler with a dietary need can do. A few phrases cover most situations. Say them, and also show them written down, since a noisy restaurant and an unfamiliar accent make spoken requests easy to mishear.


## The phrases


- "Saya alergi akan ..." means I am allergic to ...

- "Saya tidak makan ..." means I do not eat ...

- "tanpa ..." means without ...

- "Saya vegetarian" means I am vegetarian.

- "tidak pedas" means not spicy.



## The words for foods


- kacang: peanuts (also nuts and beans)

- udang: shrimp

- hasil laut: seafood

- telur: egg

- susu: milk

- babi: pork; sapi: beef; ayam: chicken; ikan: fish



## Carry a card

Our advice is to carry a printed card in Indonesian that states your allergy, says that it is serious, and lists the ingredients to avoid. Have it checked by a fluent speaker before you travel. A phrasebook sentence is a start and is no substitute for a card written for your condition.


## Vegetarian, vegan and other diets

Vegetarians and vegans eat well in Bali. Tofu, tempeh, vegetables and rice are everyday foods, and restaurants that cater to visitors are used to the request. The caution is the same as for allergies: a dish without visible meat may be built on shrimp paste or meat stock.


## Halal and kosher

Because pork is so widely used in Balinese cooking, travelers who keep halal should ask at each place and should look for restaurants that state they are halal. We have no source on kosher food in Bali and describe none; travelers who keep kosher should plan to rely on vegetarian and packaged food.


## Children

A child with an allergy needs every one of these steps taken by an adult, every meal. Pack familiar snacks for the journey and for days out. Choose accommodation with a kitchen or a restaurant willing to cook plain food to order, and speak to them before you book.


## Medication and emergencies

Bring your own medication, and enough of it. The State Department says prescription medication should be carried in its original packaging with the prescription and a doctor's letter, and declared on the arrival card. It warns that counterfeit medication is common in Indonesia, so do not plan to replace an auto-injector there.


## Import rules

Indonesia limits how much medicine a traveler may bring. Prescription medicine is allowed up to 90 days' use for prescribed medicine, backed by the prescription. Carry auto-injectors in hand luggage, with the paperwork, and keep them with you on days out.


## If a reaction happens

Use your medication as your doctor has instructed and go to a hospital. The State Department says ambulances in Indonesia can take 45 minutes or longer and that travelers may prefer a taxi or private vehicle. The emergency numbers are 112 for the unified emergency line, 110 for police, 119 for an ambulance and 113 for fire.


## Before you need it

Ask your accommodation on the first day which hospital they would send a guest to and how long it takes to get there. The State Department says hospitals often require payment or a deposit upfront. Check that your travel insurance covers your condition, and keep the assistance number in your phone.



## FAQ

How do you say "I am allergic" in Indonesian?"Saya alergi akan" followed by the food, for example "Saya alergi akan kacang" for peanuts.

Is Bali safe for people with a peanut allergy?It needs care. Peanuts are used in sauces and kitchens share oil and equipment. Carry a written card and your medication.

Is Balinese food safe for a shellfish allergy?Ask about every cooked dish. The basic Balinese spice paste usually contains shrimp paste, which is not visible.

Is it easy to be vegetarian in Bali?Yes. Tofu, tempeh and vegetables are everyday foods. Ask whether a dish is made with shrimp paste or meat stock.



===== bali-beach-clubs-how-they-work [cafes-bars-beach-clubs / None] 1211w =====
Bali Beach Clubs: Minimum Spends, Daybeds and Booking
A Bali beach club is a venue with a pool, loungers, a restaurant and a bar on or above the beach, where you pay through what you eat and drink. Many clubs set a minimum spend for a daybed or lounger instead of charging a fee for it. Policies differ from club to club and change with the season, so the way to avoid a surprise is to ask four questions before you book.


## What a beach club is

A beach club in Bali is a day venue built around a pool and a view of the sea. Guests come for the afternoon, order food and drinks, swim, and stay for sunset. The clubs are concentrated in the busy south of the island: on the west-facing coast at Seminyak and Canggu, and on the cliffs of the Bukit peninsula.


## What we can and cannot tell you

We have no official source on any club's prices, minimum spends or entry rules, and they change often, so we quote none and name no venue. What follows describes how the arrangement is commonly structured and what to check. The club's own website or a message to its reservations desk is the authority.


## How the money works

Paying at a beach club usually takes one of three forms. Some clubs let you walk in and pay only for what you order. Some charge an entry fee, which may be credited against your bill. And many set a minimum spend for the better seats, meaning you commit to ordering at least a stated amount.


## Minimum spend

A minimum spend is not a fee on top of your bill. It is a floor: you are given a daybed or a cabana on the understanding that your food and drinks will come to at least that figure. If you order less, you are charged the difference. Ask whether the figure is per person or per bed.


## Tax and service on top

The figure that matters is the one after additions. In Badung regency, which covers Seminyak, Canggu and the Bukit, the local tax on restaurant food and drink is 10%, and a venue may add a service charge. Ask whether the minimum spend is counted before or after tax and service.


## The entertainment tax question

Bars and nightclubs fall under a separate tax category. The 2023 tax regulations of Badung set that category at 40%, in force from January 2024. The rate was publicly contested and may have been amended, and we cannot tell you how any particular club is classified. Look at how the venue states its prices.



## Seats, daybeds and cabanas

Where you sit decides what you pay. A club typically has several kinds of seating: restaurant tables, bar stools, loungers by the pool, daybeds for two or more, and private cabanas. The minimum spend rises with the comfort and the view. A seat at the bar or in the restaurant often carries no minimum at all.


## What to ask about a daybed

These are our own suggestions for a message to the reservations desk.


- What is the minimum spend, and is it per person or per bed?

- Is it counted before or after tax and service?

- How many people may use the bed?

- From what time, and until when, is the bed ours?



## Time limits

A daybed may be yours for the whole day or for a set session, with a separate booking for the sunset hours. Ask. The hours before sunset are the most sought after on a west-facing coast, and a booking that ends before then is worth less to most people.


## Booking

Booking ahead matters most for sunset, for weekends and for the dry season from about May to October, when the south is busiest. Our advice is to reserve through the club's own website or official messaging account and to get the terms in writing. Walk-in guests can often still use the restaurant and bar.


## Deposits and cancellation

Some clubs ask for a deposit or a card guarantee to hold a daybed. Ask what happens to it if you cancel or if it rains. The wet season, from about November to March, brings about 9 to 14 inches of rain a month in south Bali, and a pool day is weather-dependent.


## Paying

Ask which cards are accepted and whether the bill is in rupiah. The State Department advises checking with your card issuer about international transaction fees. When a card terminal offers to charge you in dollars, our advice is to choose rupiah and let your own bank convert.


## Rules and etiquette

Beach clubs set their own dress codes and house rules, such as whether outside food and drink is allowed and whether children are admitted at all hours. Ask before you go if you are bringing children. Outside the club, Bali's rules for visitors ask for appropriate dress on the street, so cover up for the ride home.


## Drinks

The State Department warns of drink spiking in Bali and of alcohol mixed with methanol in Indonesia, and advises making sure drinks are prepared in your sight and never leaving one unattended. An established venue with sealed, branded bottles lowers the risk. Keep your drink with you.


## Getting home

Many guests leave at the same time after sunset. Our advice is to arrange the ride home before you arrive, particularly on the Bukit, which is spread out. Riding a scooter after drinking is illegal as well as dangerous; Bali's rules for visitors prohibit driving under the influence of alcohol.


## Is a beach club worth it?

A beach club suits travelers who want a pool, service and a sunset in one place, and who would spend a similar sum on lunch and drinks anyway. It suits a group splitting a daybed. Our view is that it is poor value for someone who wants only a swim.


## Cheaper ways to get the same sunset

These are our own suggestions. Book a table in the club's restaurant instead of a daybed. Go for a late lunch and stay. Or watch the sunset from the beach in front, which belongs to everyone, and come in for one drink afterward.


## One day is enough for most

Our view is that one beach club afternoon is plenty on a trip of a week or two. A second one costs as much and adds little, and the same hours could go to a temple at sunset or a walk on a quieter beach.



## FAQ

What is a minimum spend at a Bali beach club?A floor on your bill in exchange for a daybed or cabana. If you order less than the figure, you pay the difference. Ask whether it is per person or per bed.

Do Bali beach clubs charge an entry fee?Policies differ by club and change often. We quote none; check the club's own website or reservations desk.

Is tax included in beach club prices?Often not. In Badung regency the local tax on food and drink is 10%, and a service charge may be added.

Do I need to book a beach club in Bali?For a daybed at sunset, on weekends or in the dry season, booking ahead is wise. Restaurant and bar seats are easier to get.



===== indonesia-domestic-flights-baggage-and-changes [boats-and-ferries / None] 1073w =====
Indonesian Domestic Flights: Baggage Rules and Changes
Indonesian domestic flights follow different baggage rules from your international ticket, and the allowance depends on the kind of airline. Under Indonesian regulation, a full-service airline includes up to 20 kilograms of checked baggage in an economy fare, a medium-service airline up to 15, and a no-frills airline may charge for every checked bag. Read the fare conditions before you buy, and never connect a domestic flight tightly to your flight home.


## When a Bali trip includes a domestic flight

A domestic flight enters a Bali trip when the itinerary leaves the island: to Lombok for the Gili Islands, to Labuan Bajo for Komodo, to Java, or onward to another part of Indonesia. These flights are short. The planning around them is where the trouble lies, since they are separate from the ticket that brought you from the United States.


## A separate booking

A domestic flight bought independently is a separate contract from your international ticket. The US Department of Transportation's advice for any connection involving two airlines is to ask whether your bags will be transferred. On separate bookings, assume they will not, and that a delay on one flight is not the other airline's concern.


## Baggage allowances

Indonesia's Transport Ministry sorts domestic airlines into three service groups, and the checked baggage included in an economy fare depends on the group. According to Indonesian legal and business press reporting on Regulation PM 185 of 2015, full-service airlines include up to 20 kilograms, medium-service airlines up to 15, and no-frills airlines may charge.


## Why it catches Americans out

A traveler who flew to Bali with a generous international allowance may hold a domestic ticket that includes far less, or nothing. 20 kilograms is about 44 pounds and 15 kilograms about 33. Two large suitcases that crossed the Pacific without charge can cost extra on a short hop to Lombok.


## Which airline is in which group

We do not list which airline falls in which group, because classifications and fare products change, and we could not open the regulation itself to confirm the current position. The airline's own fare conditions, shown before payment, are the authority. Look for the checked baggage figure on the fare you are choosing.


## Buying baggage

Where baggage is charged, our advice is to buy it when you book, since adding it at the airport is the costly way. Weigh your bags before you leave the hotel. We quote no fees, because they vary by airline, route and when you pay.



## Carry-on and special items
